    Workbook Problem 7.5: Chemical Equilibrium Dissociation Constant (\(K_c\))

    Dinitrogen tetroxide dissociates endothermically into nitrogen dioxide gas (\(N_2 O_4 \rightleftharpoons 2 NO_2\)) in a sealed reactor with molar equilibrium constant \(K_c = 0.040\) and initial concentration \([N_2 O_4]_0 = 0.50\text{ M}\). By the Guldberg-Waage Law of Mass Action, an ICE (Initial-Change-Equilibrium) reaction extent \(x\) yields \([NO_2] = 2x\) and \([N_2 O_4] = 0.50 - x\), satisfying the quadratic equilibrium relation \(\frac{(2x)^2}{0.50 - x} = K_c\):

    \[4 x^2 + 0.040 x - 0.020 = 0\]

    Keystrokes: [↱] [POLY] 4 [A] 0.04 [B] 0.02 [+/-] [C] 0 [D] [XEQ] [RCL] 5 \(\rightarrow\) 0.0658872344

    In [↱] [POLY], entering 4 [A] 0.040 [B] loads stoichiometry coefficients \(A\) and \(B\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.0000, X: 0.0400)\). Entering 0.020 [+/-] [C] loads constant \(C = -0.020\) (with \(D\) defaulting to \(0.0\) automatically for depressed quadratic embedding, or explicitly keyed as 0 [D]) \((T: 4.0000, Z: 0.0400, Y: -0.0200, X: 0.0000)\). Striking [XEQ] evaluates quadratic roots; keying [RCL] 5 isolates the positive dissociated equilibrium extent \((T: 0.0400, Z: -0.075887, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.065887}\text{ M})\) in Register \(X\) (\(65.89\text{ mM}\), corresponding to \([NO_2] = 2x = 0.1318\text{ M}\) and \(13.18\%\) dissociation).

    Workbook Problem 7.6: Cantilever Beam Deflection Under Distributed Load

    For a statically indeterminate propped cantilever beam of span \(L = 10\text{ m}\) carrying a uniform distributed load, integrating the Euler-Bernoulli elastic curve \(EI v''(x) = M(x)\) yields a cubic compatibility polynomial whose root confirms zero deflection and slope at the clamped support:

    \[2 x^3 - 3 L x^2 + L^3 = 0 \implies \text{for } L = 10\text{ m}: \quad 2 x^3 - 30 x^2 + 1000 = 0\]

    Workbook Problem 7.7: Rocket Staging Mass Ratio Optimization

    A two-stage orbital launch vehicle with payload \(m_p = 500\text{ kg}\) and stage structural mass fractions \(\epsilon = 0.10\) must maximize total Tsiolkovsky burnout velocity \(\Delta v = c_1 \ln R_1 + c_2 \ln R_2\) under a fixed gross liftoff mass constraint. Applying Lagrange multipliers to the coupled stage mass ratios yields a symmetric quadratic optimality condition:

    \[\lambda^2 - 2 \mu \lambda + \mu = 0 \implies \lambda^2 - 4.828427 \lambda + 2.414214 = 0\]

    Keystrokes: [↱] [POLY] 1 [A] 4.828427 [+/-] [B] 2.414214 [C] 0 [D] [XEQ] [RCL] 5 \(\rightarrow\) 4.2619723651

    Workbook Problem 7.14: Hydraulic Jump Conjugate Depth Quadratic (Bélanger Equation)

    A supercritical open-channel spillway flow with upstream depth \(y_1 = 0.40\text{ m}\) and Froude number \(\text{Fr}_1 = 4.5\) (\(\text{Fr}_1^2 = 20.25\)) undergoes a turbulent stationary shock—a hydraulic jump—into tranquil subcritical flow. Although mechanical energy is dissipated across the turbulent roller, Jean-Baptiste Ch. Bélanger (1838) showed that specific momentum flux \(\frac{1}{2}y^2 + \frac{q^2}{gy}\) is conserved across the jump, yielding the Bélanger quadratic equation for downstream conjugate (sequent) depth \(y_2\): $\(y_2^2 + y_1 y_2 - 2 y_1^2 \text{Fr}_1^2 = 0 \implies y_2^2 + 0.40 y_2 - 6.48 = 0 \implies y_2 = \mathbf{2.353429\text{ meters}}\)$

Pressing [RCL] 5 recalls the positive subcritical conjugate depth \((T: 1.0000, Z: -2.7534, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{2.353429}\text{ m})\), yielding \(y_2 = 2.3534\text{ meters}\) (a \(5.88\times\) depth jump dissipating \(\Delta E = \frac{(y_2 - y_1)^3}{4 y_1 y_2} = 1.98\text{ m}\) of hydraulic head).

    Workbook Problem 7.24: Gyroscopic Rotor Nutation & Steady Precession Quadratic

    Workbook Problem 7.26: High-Speed Sprint: Cardano Cubic Instant Root

    Workbook Problem 8.1: Exponential Distribution CDF Integral

    In reliability engineering, the memoryless exponential distribution models the survival of electronic components subjected to random Poisson failure shocks. The cumulative probability that a component with unit hazard rate \(\lambda = 1\) fails before normalized time \(t = 1\) is \(F(1) = \int_0^1 e^{-x} dx\):

    \[\int_0^1 e^{-x} \, dx = 1 - e^{-1} \approx 1 - 0.3678794 = \mathbf{0.632120559}\]

    Program the hazard density into [↱] [EQN] as X [+/-] [eˣ]. Staging the unit lifetime interval 0 [ENTER] 1 \((T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0000)\) and invoking [↱] [∫] X constructs the Romberg tableau across the concave decay curve, depositing the certified Richardson truncation bound \(4.82 \times 10^{-12}\) into Register \(Y\) and the canonical \(1 - e^{-1} \approx \mathbf{0.632120559}\) (\(63.21\%\) one-time-constant failure probability) into Register \(X\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 4.82\times 10^{-12}, X: 0.632120559)\).

    Analytical Stack Verification: 1 [ENTER] 1 [+/-] [eˣ] [-] \(\implies \mathbf{0.632121}\).

    Workbook Problem 8.3: Gaussian Normal 1-Sigma Confidence Interval (\(68.27\%\))

    The Gaussian density \(\phi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}\) possesses no elementary antiderivative—a theorem proved by Joseph Liouville in 1835. Integrating across the central inflection points from \(z = -1.0\) to \(z = +1.0\) establishes the \(1\sigma\) metrological confidence band:

    \[P(-1 \le Z \le 1) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-1}^1 e^{-x^2 / 2} \, dx \approx \mathbf{0.682689492}\]

    With the standard normal curve NORM active, staging the symmetric \(1\sigma\) bounds via 1 [+/-] [ENTER] 1 (or 1 [+/-] [ENTER] [+/-]) \((T: -1.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -1.0000, X: 1.0000)\) and pressing [↱] [∫] X eliminates the inflection curvature through Richardson extrapolation, returning error bound \(1.85 \times 10^{-11}\) in Register \(Y\) and the \(68.27\%\) confidence probability \(\mathbf{0.682689492}\) in Register \(X\) \((T: -1.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 1.85\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.682689492)\).

    Workbook Problem 8.6: Polytropic Gas Expansion Work: \(P V^{1.4} = C\)

    An ideal diatomic gas expands reversibly in an insulated cylinder from \(V_1 = 0.02\text{ m}^3\) to \(V_2 = 0.08\text{ m}^3\). Initial chamber pressure \(P_1 = 600.0\text{ kPa} = 600,000\text{ Pa}\). Polytropic index \(n = 1.4\). State constant \(C = P_1 V_1^{1.4} = 600,000 \times (0.02)^{1.4} \approx 2,492.357\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{m}^{4.2}\). Boundary expansion work \(W = \int_{V_1}^{V_2} P(V) \, dV\): $\(W = C \int_{0.02}^{0.08} V^{-1.4} \, dV = \frac{P_1 V_1 \left[ 1 - (V_1/V_2)^{0.4} \right]}{0.4} = \frac{12,000 \left[ 1 - (0.25)^{0.4} \right]}{0.4}\)$ $\((0.25)^{0.4} \approx 0.57434918 \implies 1 - 0.57434918 = 0.42565082\)$ $\(W = \frac{12,000 \times 0.42565082}{0.4} = 30,000 \times 0.42565082 = \mathbf{12,769.52\text{ Joules}} = \mathbf{12.7695\text{ kJ}}\)$

    Program the adiabatic pressure law V 1.4 [+/-] [yˣ] 2492.357 [×] into [↱] [EQN]. Stage the expansion volume bounds 0.02 [ENTER] 0.08 \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0200, X: 0.0800)\) and press [↱] [∫] V \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.18\times 10^{-10}, X: 12769.5248)\). Romberg quadrature integrates the steep inverse-power pressure decay across the \(4:1\) expansion ratio, placing error bound \(2.18 \times 10^{-10}\) in Register \(Y\) and mechanical boundary work \(12,769.5248\text{ J}\) in Register \(X\), which 1000 [÷] scales to kilojoules \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.18\times 10^{-10}, X: 12.7695)\).

    Workbook Problem 8.7: Surface Area of Paraboloid Radar Dish of Revolution

    A parabolic satellite reflector dish is generated by revolving \(y = 0.25 x^2\) about the vertical \(y\)-axis from center \(x = 0\) to outer rim radius \(R = 2.0\text{ m}\). Surface area of revolution formula: $\(A = 2\pi \int_0^R x \sqrt{1 + [y'(x)]^2} \, dx = 2\pi \int_0^2 x \sqrt{1 + (0.5 x)^2} \, dx = 2\pi \int_0^2 x \sqrt{1 + 0.25 x^2} \, dx\)$ Analytical evaluation via \(u = 1 + 0.25 x^2, du = 0.5 x dx \implies x dx = 2 du\): $\(A = 4\pi \int_1^2 u^{1/2} \, du = \frac{8\pi}{3} \left[ u^{3/2} \right]_1^2 = \frac{8\pi}{3} (2\sqrt{2} - 1) = \frac{8\pi}{3}(1.8284271) \approx \mathbf{15.317764\text{ m}^2}\)$

    Workbook Problem 8.8: Thermal Heat Conduction Across Temperature-Dependent Wall

    High-temperature furnace wall of thickness \(L = 0.20\text{ m}\) and cross-sectional area \(A = 10.0\text{ m}^2\). Material thermal conductivity is temperature-dependent: \(k(T) = 0.50 + 0.002 T\text{ W/(m}\cdot\text{K)}\). Internal hot face \(T_1 = 800.0\text{ K}\), exterior cold face \(T_2 = 300.0\text{ K}\). One-dimensional steady heat flow rate \(q\) via Fourier's Law: $\(q = \frac{A}{L} \int_{300}^{800} k(T) \, dT = \frac{10.0}{0.20} \int_{300}^{800} (0.50 + 0.002 T) \, dT = 50.0 \left[ 0.50 T + 0.001 T^2 \right]_{300}^{800}\)$ $\(q = 50.0 \left[ 0.50(500) + 0.001(800^2 - 300^2) \right] = 50.0 [250 + 0.001(640,000 - 90,000)] = 50.0 [250 + 550]\)$ $\(q = 50.0 \times 800 = \mathbf{40,000.0\text{ Watts}} = \mathbf{40.000\text{ kW}}\)$

    Program Kirchhoff's integral transform T 0.002 [×] 0.5 [+] 50 [×] into [↱] [EQN].     Workbook Problem 8.10: Fresnel Cosine Integral (\(C(x)\))

    Workbook Problem 8.11: Gravitational Attraction of Thin Rod on End Particle

    A slender spacecraft boom of length \(L = 5.0\text{ m}\) and uniform mass \(M = 100.0\text{ kg}\) is positioned along the \(x\)-axis from \(x = 1.0\text{ m}\) to \(x = 6.0\text{ m}\). Newtonian attraction force \(F\): $\(F = G m \lambda \int_1^6 \frac{dx}{x^2} = (6.6743 \times 10^{-11}) \times 2.0 \times 20.0 \left[ -\frac{1}{x} \right]_1^6 = 2.66972 \times 10^{-9} \left[ 1 - \frac{1}{6} \right]\)$ $\(F = 2.66972 \times 10^{-9} \times \frac{5}{6} = \mathbf{2.224767 \times 10^{-9}\text{ Newtons}} = \mathbf{2.224767\text{ nN}}\)$

    Workbook Problem 8.12: Fourier Series 1st Harmonic Sine Coefficient (\(b_1\))

    An odd square wave of fundamental period \(2\pi\) has value \(+1\) on \((0, \pi)\) and \(-1\) on \((-\pi, 0)\). The fundamental Fourier harmonic sine coefficient \(b_1\) is: $\(b_1 = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi \sin(x) \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(x) \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2}{\pi} (1 - (-1)) = \frac{4}{\pi} \approx \mathbf{1.273240}\)$

    Workbook Problem 8.13: Sound Exposure Level (SEL) from Transient Flyover Noise

    An aircraft flyover noise pulse at an airfield boundary is modeled by acoustic pressure \(p(t) = p_0 e^{-t^2 / 20}\text{ Pa}\) with peak pressure \(p_0 = 2.00\text{ Pa}\) over \(t \in [-15, +15]\text{ s}\). Reference acoustic pressure \(p_{\text{ref}} = 20\,\mu\text{Pa} = 2.0 \times 10^{-5}\text{ Pa}\), reference time \(t_0 = 1.0\text{ s}\). Sound exposure integral \(E = \int_{-15}^{15} p^2(t) \, dt = \int_{-15}^{15} 4.0 e^{-t^2 / 10} \, dt\): $\(E \approx 4.0 \sqrt{10\pi} = 4.0 \times 5.604991 = \mathbf{22.41996\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}}\)$ $\(\text{SEL} = 10 \log_{10}\left( \frac{E}{p_{\text{ref}}^2 \cdot t_0} \right) = 10 \log_{10}\left( \frac{22.41996}{4.0 \times 10^{-10}} \right) = 10 \log_{10}(5.604991 \times 10^{10}) = \mathbf{107.4857\text{ dBA}}\)$

    Because certification standards compress an entire transient acoustic event into a 1-second equivalent energy dose, entering the squared-pressure envelope T [↰] [x²] 10 [÷] [+/-] [eˣ] 4 [×] into [↱] [EQN] and sweeping across the 30-second flyover window 15 [+/-] [ENTER] 15 \((T: 0.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: -15.0000, X: 15.0000)\) via [↱] [∫] T \((T: 0.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: 4.88\times 10^{-11}, X: 22.419964)\) integrates total sound exposure \(E = 22.419964\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}\) in Register \(X\) (with quadrature uncertainty \(4.88 \times 10^{-11}\) in \(Y\)). Normalizing against the human auditory threshold \(p_{\text{ref}}^2 t_0 = 4.0 \times 10^{-10}\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}\) and mapping to the decibel scale (4 [E] 10 [+/-] [÷] [↰] [LOG] 10 [×]) establishes the certified Sound Exposure Level \(\mathbf{107.4857\text{ dBA}}\) \((T: 4.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: 4.88\times 10^{-11}, X: 107.485743)\) (\(\text{SEL} = 107.49\text{ dBA}\)).

    Workbook Problem 8.14: Aerodynamic Circulation Line Integral & Prandtl Elliptic Spanwise Lift

    An aircraft wing of span \(b = 12.0\text{ m}\) (\(y \in [-6, +6]\text{ m}\)) flying at freestream airspeed \(V_\infty = 60.0\text{ m/s}\) in air of density \(\rho_\infty = 1.225\text{ kg/m}^3\) exhibits Prandtl's minimum-induced-drag elliptic bound circulation distribution with root vortex circulation \(\Gamma_0 = 45.0\text{ m}^2/\text{s}\):

    $\(\Gamma(y) = \Gamma_0 \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{2y}{b}\right)^2} = 45.0 \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{y}{6}\right)^2}\text{ m}^2/\text{s}\)$ By the Kutta-Joukowski theorem, sectional lift is \(L'(y) = \rho_\infty V_\infty \Gamma(y)\) (peaking at \(L'_0 = 1.225 \times 60.0 \times 45.0 = 3,307.50\text{ N/m}\) at the wing root), and total wing lift is the spanwise integral: $\(L = \rho_\infty V_\infty \int_{-6}^{6} 45.0 \sqrt{1 - (y/6)^2} \, dy = (1.225)(60.0)(45.0)\left(6\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 73.50 \times 424.1150 = \mathbf{31,172.45\text{ N}} = \mathbf{31.17\text{ kN}}\)$ Equivalent sustained aircraft mass: \(M = L / g = 31,172.45 / 9.80665 = \mathbf{3,178.71\text{ kg}} \approx \mathbf{3.18\text{ metric tonnes}}\).

    In Ludwig Prandtl's lifting-line theory, an elliptical spanwise circulation profile produces uniform downwash across the trailing vortex sheet and minimizes induced drag. Defining Y 6 [÷] [↰] [x²] 1 [x⇄y] [-] [√x] 45 [×] in [↱] [EQN] and integrating from wingtip to wingtip 6 [+/-] [ENTER] 6 \((T: 0.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: -6.0000, X: 6.0000)\) via [↱] [∫] Y \((T: 0.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 424.1150)\) yields the integrated vortex volume \(\int_{-6}^6 \Gamma(y)\,dy = 135\pi \approx 424.1150\text{ m}^3/\text{s}\). Scaling by the Kutta-Joukowski momentum flux \(\rho_\infty V_\infty\) (1.225 [×] 60 [×], \((T: 45.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 31172.4525)\)) gives total aerodynamic lift \(L = \mathbf{31,172.45\text{ N}}\) (\(\mathbf{31.17\text{ kN}}\)), which supports a steady-flight aircraft gross mass of \(\mathbf{3.18\text{ tonnes}}\) (9.80665 [÷] 1000 [÷], \((T: 45.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 3.1787)\)).

    Workbook Problem 8.15: Damped Harmonic Oscillator Energy Dissipated Per Cycle

    Viscous dashpot damper force \(F_d = c \dot{x}\) with damping coefficient \(c = 15.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{s/m}\). Natural frequency \(\omega = 20.0\text{ rad/s}\), harmonic displacement amplitude \(X_0 = 0.020\text{ m}\) (\(20\text{ mm}\)). Velocity \(v(t) = -\omega X_0 \sin(\omega t) = -0.40 \sin(20 t)\text{ m/s}\). Oscillation period \(T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{\pi}{10} \approx 0.314159\text{ s}\). Energy dissipated per steady-state cycle: $\(\Delta E = \int_0^T c v^2(t) \, dt = 15.0 \int_0^{\pi/10} [ -0.40 \sin(20 t) ]^2 dt = 2.40 \int_0^{\pi/10} \sin^2(20 t) \, dt\)$ $\(\Delta E = 2.40 \times \left( \frac{T}{2} \right) = 2.40 \times \frac{\pi}{20} = 0.12 \pi \approx \mathbf{0.376991\text{ Joules}}\)$

    Under steady-state harmonic excitation, the area enclosed by the elliptical force-displacement hysteresis loop equals the viscous thermal dissipation over one full period \(T = \pi/10\text{ s}\). With radian mode ([RAD]) active and the instantaneous power dissipation T 20 [×] [SIN] [↰] [x²] 2.4 [×] entered into [↱] [EQN], staging the single-cycle bounds 0 [↱] [π] 10 [÷] \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.314159)\) and pressing [↱] [∫] T integrates the \(\sin^2(\omega t)\) waveform to an error bound of \(1.12 \times 10^{-11}\) in \(Y\) and per-cycle thermal loss \(\mathbf{0.376991\text{ Joules}}\) in \(X\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.12\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.376991)\) (\(\Delta E = 0.3770\text{ Joules}\)).

    Workbook Problem 8.17: Second Moment of Area of Structural I-Beam Web (\(I_{xx}\))

    For a vertical steel web of height \(h = 300\text{ mm}\) (\(y \in [-150, +150]\text{ mm}\)) and thickness \(t = 10\text{ mm}\), the flexural bending stiffness depends on the second moment of area \(I_{xx}\): $\(I_{xx} = \int_{-150}^{150} t y^2 \, dy = 10 \left[ \frac{y^3}{3} \right]_{-150}^{150} = \frac{20}{3}(3,375,000) = \mathbf{22,500,000\text{ mm}^4} = 2.25 \times 10^7\text{ mm}^4\)$

    Workbook Problem 8.19: Thermal Expansion Clearance of Cryogenic Propellant Line

    Workbook Problem 8.20: Planetary Atmospheric Jeans Escape Flux

    Integrating the Maxwell-Boltzmann molecular velocity distribution above a planet's escape velocity \(v_{\text{esc}}\) (with exobase density \(n_0 = 1.0 \times 10^{13}\text{ m}^{-3}\), thermal speed \(v_{\text{th}} = 6,356.7\text{ m/s}\), and Jeans parameter \(\lambda = (v_{\text{esc}}/v_{\text{th}})^2 = 9.50\)) yields the outward molecular escape flux at the exobase: $\(F_J = \frac{n_0 v_{\text{th}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \int_{\lambda}^{\infty} u e^{-u} \, du = \frac{n_0 v_{\text{th}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}} (1 + \lambda) e^{-\lambda} = \left(1.7932 \times 10^{16}\right)(10.50) e^{-9.50} \approx \mathbf{1.4102 \times 10^{13}\text{ molecules/(m}^2\cdot\text{s)}}\)$

    In planetary aeronomy, only molecules in the high-energy Maxwell-Boltzmann tail (\(u = v^2/v_{\text{th}}^2 \ge \lambda\)) located above the collisionless exobase can escape into interplanetary space.     Workbook Problem 8.21: Torsional Rigidity of an Elliptical Shaft (Saint-Venant Warping Integral)

    Integrating Saint-Venant warping shear stress over an elliptical shaft cross-section with semiaxes \(a = 40\text{ mm}\) and \(b = 25\text{ mm}\) reduces to the normalized cross-sectional integral \(\int_{-1}^{1}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}\,du = \frac{3\pi}{8} \approx 1.178097\), scaled by \(\frac{8 a^3 b^3}{3(a^2 + b^2)}\): $\(J = \frac{8 a^3 b^3}{3(a^2 + b^2)} \int_{-1}^{1} (1 - u^2)^{3/2} \, du = \frac{\pi a^3 b^3}{a^2 + b^2} = \frac{\pi (64,000)(15,625)}{1,600 + 625} \approx \mathbf{1,411,951.76\text{ mm}^4}\)$

    Workbook Problem 8.22: Second-Order Chemical Batch Reactor Residence Time

    Workbook Problem 8.25: Supersonic Sears-Haack Minimum Wave-Drag Body

    By shaping the longitudinal cross-sectional area distribution as \(S(u) = S_{\max}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}\), the Sears-Haack body minimizes the von Kármán supersonic acoustic source integral for a fixed enclosed volume \(V\).     Workbook Problem 8.26: Stratospheric Ozone Column Integration (Dobson Units)

    Workbook Problem 8.27: High-Speed Sprint: Romberg Richardson Extrapolation on \(\int_0^4 3x^2\,dx\)

    Workbook Problem 9.1: Gaussian Normal PDF Peak at the Mean (\(Z = 0\))

    Calculate the maximum height (mode) of the normal distribution for \(\mu = 100, \sigma = 15\) at \(x = 100\):

    Calculate stock volatility Beta (\(\beta\)) against market benchmark: * Month 1: Market excess return \(x = 2.0\%\), Stock excess return \(y = 3.1\%\) * Month 2: \(x = -1.5\%, y = -2.2\%\) * Month 3: \(x = 4.0\%, y = 5.8\%\) * Month 4: \(x = -3.0\%, y = -4.4\%\)

    1. 3.1 [ENTER] 2.0 [Σ+]
    2. -2.2 [ENTER] -1.5 [Σ+]
    3. 5.8 [ENTER] 4.0 [Σ+]
    4. -4.4 [ENTER] -3.0 [Σ+]
    5. Beta \(\beta = m\): [m] \(\rightarrow\) 1.468 (High-beta growth asset)
    6. Alpha \(\alpha = b\): [b] \(\rightarrow\) 0.045%.

    In William Sharpe's Capital Asset Pricing Model, regressing an equity's excess return \(R_i - R_f\) (\(Y\)) against the market portfolio's excess return \(R_m - R_f\) (\(X\)) partitions total return into systematic market sensitivity \(\beta = \text{Cov}(R_i, R_m)/\text{Var}(R_m)\) ([m], \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{1.4676})\)) and idiosyncratic risk-adjusted outperformance, or Jensen's Alpha \(\alpha\) ([b] \(= 0.045\%\)).

    Workbook Problem 9.22: Vector Graphic Scatter Plotting & Trendline Overlay

    Accessible in App Mode via Right Shift [↱] then [PLOT] (excluded from physical firmware for standardized-exam compliance)

    When bivariate Σ+ data is accumulated and no algebraic equations are stored in EQN, pressing [↱] [PLOT] in App Mode transitions the high-contrast dot-matrix display to the bivariate scatter & regression plotter: * The independent variable \(x\) scales along the horizontal axis (\(X_{\min} \to X_{\max}\)). * The dependent variable \(y\) scales along the vertical axis (\(Y_{\min} \to Y_{\max}\)). * Each accumulated point appears as a \(2\times 2\) pixel reticle. * The linear regression line \(y = m x + b\) is rendered as an uninterrupted 1-pixel vector trendline across the display field. * Navigation: * Press [4] to pan left, [6] to pan right. * Press [8] to pan up, [2] to pan down. * Press [+] to zoom in, [-] to zoom out. * Press [C] to exit plot mode and return to the RPN stack.

    As Francis Anscombe famously demonstrated with Anscombe's Quartet, summary regression statistics (\(m, b, r\)) alone cannot distinguish a true linear relationship from high-leverage outliers or unmodeled curvature. Launching [↱] [PLOT] in App Mode overlays the least-squares vector trendline \(y = mx + b\) directly across the \(2\times 2\) pixel data reticles while preserving the operational stack registers \((T, Z, Y, X)\) untouched in background memory; panning ([4]/[6], [8]/[2]) and zooming ([+]/[-]) inspect residual structure visually before [C] returns to the four-register stack \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0000)\).

    Workbook Problem 9.23: Residual Analysis and Standard Error of the Estimate (\(s_e\))

    In linear regression \(y = m x + b\), the standard error of the estimate measures the dispersion of observed values around the regression line:

    \[s_e = \sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{n - 2}} = \sqrt{\frac{\Sigma y^2 - b \Sigma y - m \Sigma xy}{n - 2}}\]

    For the pressure sensor dataset from Textbook Showcase Problem 9.1: * \(n = 4\), degrees of freedom \(n - 2 = 2\) * \(\Sigma y = 12.45\), \(\Sigma y^2 = 46.5075\), \(\Sigma xy = 373.5\) * \(m = 0.1245\), \(b = 0.0000\)

    Compute residual sum of squares: $\(\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = 46.5075 - 0.0000(12.45) - 0.1245(373.5) = 46.5075 - 46.50075 = 0.00675\text{ V}^2\)$ $\(s_e = \sqrt{\frac{0.00675}{2}} = \sqrt{0.003375} \approx 0.05809\text{ V}\)$

    Keystrokes: 46.5075 [ENTER] .1245 [ENTER] 373.5 [×] [-] 2 [÷] [√x] \(\rightarrow\) 0.0580947502

    Because the six statistical registers already hold the exact cross-product moments, the residual sum of squares \(\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = \Sigma y^2 - b\Sigma y - m\Sigma xy\) can be evaluated without re-visiting individual observations (\(b = 0\) here). Staging \(\Sigma y^2\) via 46.5075 [ENTER] \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 46.5075, X: 46.5075)\) and subtracting the regression sum of squares \(m \Sigma xy\) via 0.1245 [ENTER] 373.5 [×] [-] isolates \(\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = 0.00675\text{ V}^2\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.00675)\); normalizing by \(n - 2 = 2\) degrees of freedom (2 [÷], \(s_e^2 = 0.003375\)) and taking the square root ([√x]) determines the root-mean-square calibration scatter \(s_e = \mathbf{0.0580947502\text{ V}}\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0580947502)\).

    Workbook Problem 9.24: Binomial Distribution Probability via Combinatorics [Cn,r]

    In an automated manufacturing testing line, component defect probability is \(p = 0.03\). In a batch of \(n = 20\) components, calculate the probability of observing exactly \(k = 2\) defective units: $\(P(X = 2) = \binom{20}{2} (0.03)^2 (0.97)^{18}\)$

    Keystrokes: 20 [ENTER] 2 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] .03 [↰] [x²] [×] .97 [ENTER] 18 [yˣ] [×] \(\rightarrow\) 0.0988; 0.1710 [ENTER] .97 [ENTER] 18 [yˣ] [×] \(\rightarrow\) 0.0988304453

    In Bernoulli lot acceptance sampling, the number of distinct defect pairings across \(n = 20\) items is counted by the binomial coefficient 20 [ENTER] 2 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] (\(\binom{20}{2} = \mathbf{190}\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 190.0000)\)). Multiplying by the defective pair probability \(p^2\) via .03 [↰] [x²] [×] (\(190 \times 0.0009 = 0.1710\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.1710)\)) and weighting by the survival probability of the remaining 18 good units (.97 [ENTER] 18 [yˣ] [×], \(0.97^{18} \approx 0.5779\)) resolves the exact binomial mass \(P(X = 2) = \mathbf{0.0988304453}\) (\(9.88\%\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0988304453)\).

    Workbook Problem 9.25: Hypergeometric Sampling Without Replacement

    In a batch of \(N = 50\) aerospace fasteners, \(K = 6\) are defective. A quality inspector selects \(n = 5\) fasteners at random without replacement. Find the probability that exactly \(k = 1\) selected fastener is defective: $\(P(X = 1) = \frac{\binom{K}{k} \binom{N - K}{n - k}}{\binom{N}{n}} = \frac{\binom{6}{1} \binom{44}{4}}{\binom{50}{5}}\)$

    Keystrokes: 6 [ENTER] 1 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] 44 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [×] 50 [ENTER] 5 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [÷]; 6 [ENTER] 44 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [×]; 50 [ENTER] 5 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 0.384426

    When sampling a finite aerospace lot without replacement (\(n/N = 10\%\)), each draw alters the defect concentration of the remaining population, requiring the exact hypergeometric ratio of combinatorial counts. Evaluating 6 [ENTER] 1 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] (\(\binom{6}{1} = 6.0000\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 6.0000)\)), multiplying by the conforming combinations 44 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [×] (\(\binom{44}{4} = 135,751 \implies 814,506\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 814506.0000)\)), and dividing by the total sample space 50 [ENTER] 5 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] [÷] (\(\binom{50}{5} = 2,118,760\)) determines \(P(X = 1) = \mathbf{0.384426}\) (\(38.44\%\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.384426)\).

    Workbook Problem 9.26: Log-Normal Aerosol Droplet Size Distribution

    In medical nebulizer sprays, aerosol droplet diameters \(D\) follow a log-normal distribution with geometric mean \(\mu_{\ln} = \ln(4.5\text{ }\mu\text{m}) = 1.504077\) and geometric standard deviation \(\sigma_{\ln} = 0.35\). Calculate the probability density for a droplet of diameter \(D = 5.0\text{ }\mu\text{m}\): $\(f(D) = \frac{1}{D \sigma_{\ln} \sqrt{2\pi}} \exp\left( -\frac{(\ln D - \mu_{\ln})^2}{2\sigma_{\ln}^2} \right)\)$

    Keystrokes: 5.0 [LN] 1.504077 [-] [↰] [x²] .35 [↰] [x²] 2 [×] [÷] [+/-] [eˣ] 5.0 [ENTER] .35 [×] 2 [↱] [π] [×] [√x] [×] [÷]; 0.0111009219 [ENTER] .35 [↰] [x²] 2 [×] [÷] [+/-] [eˣ]; 0.955701 [ENTER] 5.0 [ENTER] .35 [×] 2 [↱] [π] [×] [√x] [×] [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 0.217865884

    Because multiplicative breakup of liquid sheets in a pneumatic nebulizer produces normal scatter in \(\ln D\) rather than \(D\), the Jacobian transformation \(d(\ln D)/dD = 1/D\) introduces an explicit diameter prefactor into the log-normal density. Evaluating the squared log-ratio 5.0 [LN] 4.5 [LN] [-] [↰] [x²] (\((\ln 5.0 - \ln 4.5)^2 \approx 0.011101\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.011101)\)), dividing by \(2\sigma_{\ln}^2\) and exponentiating via .35 [↰] [x²] 2 [×] [÷] [+/-] [eˣ] (\(\exp(-0.045310) \approx 0.955701\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.955701)\)), and normalizing by \(D\sigma_{\ln}\sqrt{2\pi}\) (5.0 [ENTER] .35 [×] 2 [↱] [π] [×] [√x] [×] [÷]) resolves \(f(5.0) = \mathbf{0.217865884\ \mu\text{m}^{-1}}\) \((T: 0.9557, Z: 0.9557, Y: 0.9557, X: 0.217865884)\).

    Chapter 10: Computer Science, Bases & 36-Bit Integers — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems

    Workbook Problem 10.1: IPv4 CIDR /27 Subnet (30 Usable Hosts)

    A server has IP 10.20.30.142 /27. * \(10 = \text{0Ah}, 20 = \text{14h}, 30 = \text{1Eh}, 142 = \text{8Eh} \implies \text{0A141E8Eh}\). * Subnet Mask /27: \(27\) ones, \(5\) zeros \(\implies\) block size \(2^5 = 32_{10} = \text{20h}\) (\(255.255.255.224\)).

    Octet 4 Representation Upper 3 Subnet Bits Lower 5 Host Bits Hex / Decimal Value
    Host Octet 4 100 01110 8Eh (\(142_{10}\))
    Subnet Mask (/27) 111 00000 E0h (\(224_{10}\))
    Network Base (÷R 20h × 20h) 100 00000 80h (\(128_{10}\))
    Broadcast (+ 1Fh) 100 11111 9Fh (\(159_{10}\))

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 1. 0A141E8E [ENTER] 20 [↱] [÷R] 20 [×] \(\rightarrow\) A141E80h (10.20.30.128) 2. Wildcard mask: \(32 - 27 = 5\) bits \(\implies 2^5 - 1 = 31_{10} = \text{1Fh}\). 3. Broadcast: 0A141E80 [ENTER] 1F [+] \(\rightarrow\) A141E9Fh (10.20.30.159) 4. Usable host range: 10.20.30.129 through 10.20.30.158 (\(30\) usable hosts).

    In CIDR block allocation, a /27 prefix reserves 27 upper network bits and leaves \(32 - 27 = 5\) host bits, partitioning each /24 into eight 32-address (20h) blocks. Staging the packed address 0A141E8E [ENTER] (10.20.30.142) in [HEX] mode \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{A141E8Eh}, X: \text{A141E8Eh})\) and dividing by the block stride 20 [↱] [÷R] splits the 27-bit network prefix quotient \(\text{50A0F4h}\) into Register \(X\) and the 5-bit host index \(\text{Eh}\) (\(14_{10}\)) into Register \(Y\) \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{Eh}, X: \text{50A0F4h})\). Multiplying by the stride 20 [×] aligns the network base A141E80h (10.20.30.128), and adding the 5-bit wildcard mask 1F [+] (\(2^5 - 1 = 31_{10}\)) establishes the directed broadcast address \(\mathbf{\text{A141E9Fh}}\) (10.20.30.159).

    Workbook Problem 10.2: IPv4 Point-to-Point /30 Subnet (WAN Link)

    A WAN router serial link connects two routers using 172.16.5.9 /30. * \(172 = \text{ACh}, 16 = \text{10h}, 5 = \text{05h}, 9 = \text{09h} \implies \text{AC100509h}\). * Subnet Mask /30: \(30\) ones, \(2\) zeros \(\implies\) block size \(2^2 = 4_{10} = \text{4h}\) (\(255.255.255.252\)).

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 1. AC100509 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [÷R] 4 [×] \(\rightarrow\) AC100508h (172.16.5.8) 2. Wildcard: \(2^2 - 1 = 3 = \text{03h}\). 3. Broadcast: AC100508 [ENTER] 3 [+] \(\rightarrow\) AC10050Bh (172.16.5.11) 4. Router A interface: 172.16.5.9, Router B interface: 172.16.5.10 (\(2\) usable hosts).

    Point-to-point WAN links traditionally use /30 prefixes (stride \(2^2 = 4\text{h}\)) to conserve IPv4 address space while providing exactly two routable endpoints between network and broadcast boundaries. Entering AC100509 [ENTER] in [HEX] mode and partitioning with 4 [↱] [÷R] separates host offset \(1\text{h}\) in Register \(Y\) from the 30-bit prefix quotient \(\text{2B040142h}\) in Register \(X\) \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 1\text{h}, X: \text{2B040142h})\); scaling back by 4 [×] recovers the wire network ID \(\mathbf{\text{AC100508h}}\) (172.16.5.8), and adding the 2-bit wildcard 3 [+] determines the link broadcast address \(\mathbf{\text{AC10050Bh}}\) (172.16.5.11).

    Workbook Problem 10.3: IPv4 Host Route (/32) and Loopback

    A BGP loopback interface is assigned 198.51.100.1 /32. * In Hex: IP is C6336401h, Mask is FFFFFFFFh (\(255.255.255.255\)). * Network ID = Host IP = Broadcast = 198.51.100.1 (C6336401h). * Usable hosts: \(2^{32-32} = 2^0 = \mathbf{1\text{ host}}\).

    Keystrokes in [HEX] & [DEC] Modes: 1. Load Host IP: C6336401 [ENTER] \(\rightarrow\) C6336401h 2. Wildcard mask: FFFFFFFF [ENTER] FFFFFFFF [-] \(\rightarrow\) 0h (0.0.0.0) 3. Broadcast Address: [+] \(\rightarrow\) C6336401h (198.51.100.1) 4. Host Capacity: Switch to [DEC], 2 [ENTER] 0 [yˣ] \(\rightarrow\) 1.

    Autonomous system border routers advertise /32 loopback interfaces into BGP and OSPF so routing tables track a virtual node that never goes down when a single physical port flaps. With C6336401 [ENTER] staged on the stack \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{C6336401h}, X: \text{C6336401h})\), complementing the 32-bit host mask (FFFFFFFF [ENTER] FFFFFFFF [-]) yields a zero wildcard offset 0h, confirming via [+] that network, host, and broadcast collapse into the single address \(\mathbf{\text{C6336401h}}\) (\(2^0 = \mathbf{1}\) via [↰] [BASE] [DEC] 2 [ENTER] 0 [yˣ]).

    Workbook Problem 10.4: CIDR Route Aggregation (Supernetting)

    An ISP wants to summarize four contiguous subnets into a single route: * Subnet 1: 192.168.16.0 /24 (C0A81000h) * Subnet 2: 192.168.17.0 /24 (C0A81100h) * Subnet 3: 192.168.18.0 /24 (C0A81200h) * Subnet 4: 192.168.19.0 /24 (C0A81300h)

    Inspect the third octet: \(16 = 00010000_2\), \(17 = 00010001_2\), \(18 = 00010010_2\), \(19 = 00010011_2\). The upper 6 bits (\(000100_2\)) are identical across all four routes! New network mask length: \(16 + 6 = \mathbf{22\text{ bits}}\) (/22). Supernet Mask: 255.255.252.0 (FFFFFC00h). Summarized Route: 192.168.16.0 /22.

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 1. Base Network ID: C0A81000 [ENTER] \(\rightarrow\) C0A81000h (192.168.16.0) 2. Supernet block span: 4 [ENTER] 100 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 400h (\(1024_{10}\) addresses) 3. Wildcard Mask: 1 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 3FFh (0.0.3.255) 4. Broadcast Address: [+] \(\rightarrow\) C0A813FFh (192.168.19.255) 5. Usable Hosts: 3FF [ENTER] 1 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 3FEh (\(1022_{10}\) hosts).

    CIDR route aggregation (RFC 4632) prevents global routing-table explosion by folding contiguous power-of-two /24 blocks into a shorter prefix. Staging the aligned base network C0A81000 [ENTER] and evaluating the 4-block span 4 [ENTER] 100 [×] (400h \(= 1024_{10}\) addresses, \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: \text{C0A81000h}, Y: 4\text{h}, X: 400\text{h})\)) followed by 1 [-] derives the 10-bit supernet wildcard mask 3FFh (0.0.3.255). Adding the wildcard to the waiting base address via [+] resolves the summarized broadcast boundary \(\mathbf{\text{C0A813FFh}}\) (192.168.19.255), while 3FF [ENTER] 1 [-] confirms \(\mathbf{\text{3FEh}}\) (\(1022_{10}\)) usable host addresses.

    Workbook Problem 10.5: Wildcard Mask Generation via 32-Bit Complement Subtraction

    In Cisco ACLs, calculate the wildcard mask for /26 (\(255.255.255.192\)): 1. In [HEX]: Mask is FFFFFFC0h. 2. Subtract from the 32-bit all-ones mask FFFFFFFFh: key FFFFFFFF [ENTER] FFFFFFC0 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 3Fh. 3. In [DEC]: 3Fh = 63. 4. Dotted decimal wildcard: 0.0.0.63.

    Because a bitwise one's complement on an \(N\)-bit unsigned word is algebraically identical to subtracting from \(2^N - 1\), subtracting the /26 netmask FFFFFFC0h from the 32-bit all-ones constant FFFFFFFF [ENTER] \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{FFFFFFFFh}, X: \text{FFFFFFFFh})\) via FFFFFFC0 [-] \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{3Fh})\) isolates the inverted access-control wildcard 3Fh, which [↰] [BASE] [DEC] displays as \(\mathbf{63}\) (0.0.0.63).

    Workbook Problem 10.6: Embedded GPIO Pin Bit-Clearing (-)

    Clear Bit 11 (Disable motor enable line, currently \(1\)) in register holding 0x00002F80. Bit 11 weight: \(2^{11} = 2048_{10} = \text{800h}\).

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 2F80 [ENTER] 800 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 2780h

    When a target bit \(k\) is known to be asserted (\(1\)), subtracting its positional weight \(2^k\) executes a borrow-free bitwise clear (R & ~(1 << k)). Staging 2F80 [ENTER] in [HEX] mode and subtracting Bit 11's weight \(2^{11} = \text{800h}\) via 800 [-] clears the motor-enable bit cleanly without disturbing adjacent flags, leaving \(\mathbf{\text{2780h}}\) in Register \(X\) \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{2780h})\).

    Workbook Problem 10.7: Multi-Bit Field Insertion (Read-Modify-Write)

    In a peripheral timer control register currently holding 0x4521, update the 4-bit prescaler field at bits [11:8] (currently 5h) with value 0xA (\(10_{10}\)), without altering any of the surrounding configuration bits.

    Insertion Stage Binary Nibbles (Bits 15–0) Hexadecimal Value
    Original Timer Register 0100 0101 0010 0001 4521h
    Subtract Old Field (- 500h) 0100 0000 0010 0001 4021h
    Shifted New Field (A × 100h) 0000 1010 0000 0000 0A00h
    Updated Register (+) 0100 1010 0010 0001 4A21h

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 1. Clear existing field: 4521 [ENTER] 500 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 4021h 2. Prepare shifted new field: A [ENTER] 100 [×] \(\rightarrow\) A00h 3. Merge fields: [+] \(\rightarrow\) 4A21h

    Firmware read-modify-write sequences (R = (R & ~MASK) | (VAL << SHIFT)) translate cleanly onto the hexadecimal stack: subtracting the old field 4521 [ENTER] 500 [-] zeroes bits [11:8] to leave the masked register 4021h in Register \(X\) \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{4021h})\), left-shifting the new nibble via A [ENTER] 100 [×] forms A00h in \(X\) while 4021h waits in \(Y\) \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{4021h}, X: \text{A00h})\), and pressing [+] merges the updated control word \(\mathbf{\text{4A21h}}\).

    Workbook Problem 10.8: RGB565 to RGB888 Color Unpacking & Expansion

    Given packed RGB565 word 0xE5B2, extract individual color channels using [÷] and [↱] [÷R] and expand them to standard 8-bit values (\(0 \dots 255\)): * Red channel (bits 15–11): divide by \(2^{11} = \text{800h}\) * Green channel (bits 10–5): take remainder modulo \(\text{800h}\) via [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y], then divide by \(2^5 = \text{20h}\) * Blue channel (bits 4–0): take remainder modulo \(\text{20h}\) via [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y]

    Keystrokes in [HEX] & [DEC] Modes: 1. Extract Red: E5B2 [ENTER] 800 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 1Ch (\(28_{10}\)) * Expand in [DEC]: 28 [ENTER] 255 [×] 31 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 230.32258065 2. Extract Green: E5B2 [ENTER] 800 [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y] 20 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 2Dh (\(45_{10}\)) * Expand in [DEC]: 45 [ENTER] 255 [×] 63 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 182.14285714 3. Extract Blue: E5B2 [ENTER] 20 [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y] \(\rightarrow\) 12h (\(18_{10}\)) * Expand in [DEC]: 18 [ENTER] 255 [×] 31 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 148.06451613 * Unpacked TrueColor: RGB(230, 182, 148) (#E6B694).

    Because [↱] [÷R] simultaneously returns the upper bitfield quotient in Register \(X\) and the lower bitfield remainder in Register \(Y\), a 16-bit RGB565 pixel can be unpacked either channel-by-channel (E5B2 [ENTER] 800 [÷] \(\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{1Ch}} = 28_{10} \implies R = \mathbf{230}\); E5B2 [ENTER] 800 [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y] 20 [÷] \(\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{2Dh}} = 45_{10} \implies G = \mathbf{182}\); E5B2 [ENTER] 20 [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y] \(\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{12h}} = 18_{10} \implies B = \mathbf{148}\)) or in a single-entry cascade: keying E5B2 [ENTER] 800 [↱] [÷R] [x⇄y] 20 [↱] [÷R] unpacks all three channels onto the stack at once (Z: 1Ch Red, Y: 12h Blue, X: 2Dh Green) with E5B2 typed only once!

    Workbook Problem 10.9: RGBA4444 16-Bit Texture Color Packing

    In embedded OpenGL ES graphics engines, 16-bit RGBA4444 allocates 4 bits per channel: Pack channels \(R=14\) (Eh), \(G=9\) (9h), \(B=3\) (3h), \(A=15\) (Fh): $\((R \times \text{1000h}) + (G \times \text{100h}) + (B \times \text{10h}) + A\)$

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: E [ENTER] 1000 [×] 9 [ENTER] 100 [×] [+] 3 [ENTER] 10 [×] [+] F [+] \(\rightarrow\) E93Fh

    In 4-bit-per-channel RGBA4444 sprite textures, each channel aligns with a single hexadecimal digit weight (\(16^3, 16^2, 16^1, 16^0\)). Shifting Red via E [ENTER] 1000 [×] (\(\text{E000h}\), \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{E000h})\)) and accumulating Green (9 [ENTER] 100 [×] [+] \(\rightarrow \text{E900h}\)), Blue (3 [ENTER] 10 [×] [+] \(\rightarrow \text{E930h}\)), and Alpha (F [+]) assembles the packed 16-bit texel \(\mathbf{\text{E93Fh}}\) in Register \(X\).

    Workbook Problem 10.10: Two's Complement Negation Proof (\((2^{36} - 1 - X) + 1\))

    Verify that subtracting \(X\) from all ones (FFFFFFFFFh, 9 hex digits for 36 bits) and adding \(1\) produces the exact mathematical two's complement negation [+/-]: * Key in \(42\) in [DEC] mode and press [+/-]: produces -42. * Switch to [↰] [BASE] [HEX]: displays as FFFFFFFD6h (\(36\)-bit two's complement representation, \(2^{36} - 42\)). * Verify by one's complement subtraction: FFFFFFFFF [ENTER] 2A [-] 1 [+] \(\rightarrow\) FFFFFFFD6h (-42 in [DEC]).

    In an \(N\)-bit two's complement ALU (\(N = 36\) on the SC-32), additive inverse is defined modulo \(2^{36}\) via \(-X \equiv (2^{36} - 1 - X) + 1 \pmod{2^{36}}\). Negating 42 [+/-] in [DEC] mode yields \(\mathbf{-42}\), whose underlying 36-bit word appears in [↰] [BASE] [HEX] as \(\mathbf{\text{FFFFFFFD6h}}\)—identical to forming the 9-nibble one's complement FFFFFFFFF [ENTER] 2A [-] (\(\text{FFFFFFFD5h}\)) and incrementing by the carry-in 1 [+].

    Workbook Problem 10.11: Arithmetic Right Shift via Signed Integer Division

    For signed negative integer \(-64\): * In [DEC]: 64 [+/-] [ENTER] 4 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) -16 (Exact arithmetic right shift by 2 bits, sign-extended).

    Whereas a logical right shift (>>) pads vacated upper bits with zeros, an arithmetic right shift (>>> or ASR) replicates the sign bit (Bit 35) to preserve negative two's complement magnitudes. Staging 64 [+/-] [ENTER] in [DEC] mode and dividing by \(2^2 = 4\) via 4 [÷] yields \(\mathbf{-16}\) \((T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: -16)\), which [↰] [BASE] [HEX] confirms as the sign-extended 36-bit word \(\mathbf{\text{FFFFFFFFF0h}}\).

    Workbook Problem 10.12: Parity Bit Generation

    Data byte 0x3B (\(00111011_2\), five ones \(\implies\) odd parity). For even parity, the MSB parity bit (Bit 7, weight \(2^7 = 128_{10} = \text{80h}\)) must be set to \(1\): 3B [ENTER] 80 [+] \(\rightarrow\) BBh.

    Because the 7-bit ASCII payload 3Bh (\(00111011_2\)) contains an odd population count of five 1 bits, asserting the MSB parity bit (\(2^7 = \text{80h}\)) via 3B [ENTER] 80 [+] brings total population weight to six 1 bits, forming the even-parity UART frame byte \(\mathbf{\text{BBh}}\) (\(10111011_2\)).

    Workbook Problem 10.13: Longitudinal Redundancy Check (LRC)

    Calculate LRC across message bytes 0x02, 0x41, 0x42.

    2 [ENTER] 41 [+] 42 [+] \(\rightarrow\) 85h.

    LRC = \((256 - \text{Sum}) \bmod 256\): 100 [ENTER] 85 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 7Bh.

    In Intel HEX and Modbus ASCII serial framing, the Longitudinal Redundancy Check (LRC) byte is the two's complement of the modulo-256 payload sum, ensuring that the sum of payload plus LRC equals 00h \((\bmod\text{ 100h})\) at the receiver. Summing the three frame bytes 2 [ENTER] 41 [+] 42 [+] yields 85h \((T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{85h})\), and subtracting from 100h (100 [ENTER] 85 [-], or 100 [x⇄y] [-] without re-typing 85) generates the transmitted LRC byte \(\mathbf{\text{7Bh}}\).

    Workbook Problem 10.14: Ethernet FCS 32-Bit Inversion & Frame Check Sequence Post-Conditioning

    In IEEE 802.3 Ethernet frame transmission, the final 32-bit CRC remainder (Frame Check Sequence, FCS) is post-conditioned prior to transmission by inverting all 32 bits (one's complement). Given an Ethernet polynomial check state of 0xEDB88320: 1. Invert the 32-bit word by subtracting from the all-ones mask FFFFFFFFh: $\(FFFFFFFF_{16} - EDB88320_{16} = \mathbf{12477CDF}_{16} \ (306,678,751_{10})\)$

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 1. Load 32-bit inversion mask: FFFFFFFF [ENTER] \(\rightarrow\) FFFFFFFFh 2. Subtract Ethernet generator polynomial: EDB88320 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 12477CDFh 3. Switch radix to inspect in decimal: [↰] [BASE] [DEC] \(\rightarrow\) 306678751.

    In IEEE 802.3 CRC-32 hardware, post-inverting the shift-register state against FFFFFFFFh ensures that appending trailing zero bytes to a valid frame alters its CRC. Subtracting the reversed generator polynomial EDB88320h from FFFFFFFF [ENTER] via EDB88320 [-] produces the one's-complement word \(\mathbf{\text{12477CDFh}}\), which [↰] [BASE] [DEC] renders losslessly as \(\mathbf{306,678,751}\).

    Workbook Problem 10.15: SPI Clock Prescaler Selection

    A SPI peripheral runs from a \(75.0\text{ MHz}\) bus clock. The connected flash memory specifies a maximum operating frequency of \(10.0\text{ MHz}\). The SPI prescaler offers powers of two: \(2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128\). Determine the optimal prescaler divider and actual SPI operating frequency.

    Keystrokes: 75 [ENTER] 10 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) \(7.5\).

    Dividing the \(75.0\text{ MHz}\) peripheral bus clock by the \(10.0\text{ MHz}\) NOR-flash ceiling yields the minimum divider ratio \(7.5000\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7.5000)\). Rounding up to the next binary prescaler tap \(2^{\lceil \log_2 7.5 \rceil} = \mathbf{8}\) (8 [ENTER]) and dividing the bus clock via 75 [ENTER] 8 [÷] establishes the safe operating SPI clock \(f_{\text{SPI}} = \mathbf{9.375\text{ Hz}}\) (\(9.375\text{ MHz}\)).

    Workbook Problem 10.16: I2C Fast-Mode Plus SCL Timing Register Calculations

    Target I2C clock frequency \(f_{\text{SCL}} = 1.0\text{ MHz}\) (\(1000\text{ ns}\) cycle time) derived from a \(150.0\text{ MHz}\) core clock period (\(T_{\text{clk}} = \frac{1}{150\times 10^6} \approx 6.6667\text{ ns}\)). Total clocks per SCL period: \(150\text{ cycles}\). In Fast-Mode Plus, asymmetric duty cycle allocates \(t_{\text{LOW}} \ge 500\text{ ns}\) and \(t_{\text{HIGH}} \ge 260\text{ ns}\). Allocating \(\frac{1}{3}\) high and \(\frac{2}{3}\) low: * \(t_{\text{HIGH}} = 50\text{ cycles} \times 6.6667\text{ ns} = \mathbf{333.3\text{ ns}}\) (\(> 260\text{ ns}\)) * \(t_{\text{LOW}} = 100\text{ cycles} \times 6.6667\text{ ns} = \mathbf{666.7\text{ ns}}\) (\(> 500\text{ ns}\)).

    Keystrokes: 1. Total cycles: 150 [ENTER] 1 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) \(150\) cycles 2. High phase clocks: 3 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) \(50\) cycles 3. Timing durations: 1000 [ENTER] 3 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 333.3 ns (\(t_{\text{HIGH}}\)); 2 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 666.7 ns (\(t_{\text{LOW}}\)).

    Open-drain I2C buses require an asymmetric 1:2 high-to-low clock ratio at \(1\text{ MHz}\) (Fast-Mode Plus) so active pull-down transistors hold SCL low long enough (\(t_{\text{LOW}} \ge 500\text{ ns}\)) after passive pull-up RC rise times. Dividing the \(150\text{-cycle}\) period (150 [ENTER] 1 [÷]) by 3 [÷] assigns \(50\text{ cycles}\) to SCLL_H and \(100\text{ cycles}\) to SCLL_L; partitioning the \(1000\text{ ns}\) bit period (1000 [ENTER] 3 [÷]) confirms \(t_{\text{HIGH}} = \mathbf{333.3\text{ ns}} > 260\text{ ns}\) and 2 [×] confirms \(t_{\text{LOW}} = \mathbf{666.7\text{ ns}} > 500\text{ ns}\).

    Workbook Problem 10.17: Fixed-Point Q16.16 Arithmetic Multiplication

    In digital signal processing without FPU acceleration, numbers are formatted as signed Q16.16 (16 integer bits, 16 fractional bits, scaling factor \(2^{16} = 65,536\)). Multiply \(A = 3.5\) and \(B = 2.25\): * \(A = 3.5 \times 65536 = 229,376 = \text{38000h}\) * \(B = 2.25 \times 65536 = 147,456 = \text{24000h}\) * Product formula: \(C = (A \times B) \gg 16 = (A \times B) / 65536\).

    Keystrokes in [HEX] Mode: 38000 [ENTER] 24000 [×] 10000 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 7E000h

    Because multiplying two Q16.16 fixed-point numbers \((A \cdot 2^{16})(B \cdot 2^{16}) = AB \cdot 2^{32}\) doubles the binary-point scaling to Q32.32, a 32-bit ALU would overflow on the intermediate product \(\text{38000h} \times \text{24000h} = \text{7E0000000h}\). The SC-32's 36-bit integer datapath accommodates the full 35-bit intermediate product (38000 [ENTER] 24000 [×]) without truncation, and right-shifting by 16 bits (10000 [÷]) normalizes the result back to Q16.16 word \(\mathbf{\text{7E000h}}\) (\(516,096_{10} / 65,536 = \mathbf{7.875}\)).

    Workbook Problem 10.18: Permutations (\(nPr\)) in Cryptography S-Box Design

    Calculate the total number of ways to construct an ordered 4-element substitution key from a pool of 16 distinct nibbles:

    \[P(16, 4) = \frac{16!}{(16 - 4)!} = 16 \times 15 \times 14 \times 13 = \mathbf{43,680}\]

    Keystrokes: 16 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [PROB] [Pn,r] \(\rightarrow\) 43,680

    In block-cipher substitution-box (S-Box) construction, selecting an ordered sequence of \(r = 4\) distinct 4-bit nibbles without replacement from the 16-element hexadecimal alphabet spans the falling factorial \(16^{\underline{4}} = P(16, 4)\). Keying 16 [ENTER] 4 [↱] [PROB] [Pn,r] evaluates the exact keyspace cardinality \(P(16, 4) = \mathbf{43,680}\) (\(43,680\)).

    Workbook Problem 10.19: Combinations (\(nCr\)) and Hamming Sphere

    In a \([7, 4]\) Hamming code, calculate the number of possible 2-bit error patterns:

    \[\binom{7}{2} = \frac{7!}{2! \times 5!} = \mathbf{21}\]

    Keystrokes: 7 [ENTER] 2 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] \(\rightarrow\) 21

    In Richard Hamming's \([7, 4]\) single-error-correcting block code, the surface area of the Hamming sphere of radius \(t = 2\) around any 7-bit codeword counts all unordered pairs of corrupted bit positions. Evaluating 7 [ENTER] 2 [↱] [PROB] [Cn,r] returns \(\binom{7}{2} = \mathbf{21}\) (\(21\) distinct double-bit error patterns).

    Workbook Problem 10.20: Factorials and Stirling's Approximation Comparison

    Calculate \(15!\): Exact: 15 [↱] [PROB] [↰] [x!] \(\rightarrow\) 1,307,674,368,000.

    Stirling's approximation: \(\sqrt{2\pi n} (n/e)^n \approx 1.3004 \times 10^{12}\) (Error \(< 0.6\%\)).

    Evaluating 15 [↱] [PROB] [↰] [x!] computes the exact permutation count of a 15-element array, \(\mathbf{1,307,674,368,000}\) (\(1,307,674,368,000\)), whereas James Stirling's asymptotic leading term \(\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^n \approx 1.30043 \times 10^{12}\) underestimates the exact factorial by the series correction factor \(e^{-1/(12n)} \approx 1 - \frac{1}{180}\) (\(0.55\%\)).

    Workbook Problem 10.21: Pseudo-Random Number Generation (RAND)

    Generate 3 uniform random floats: [↱] [PROB] [RAND] \(\rightarrow\) \(0.724194\) [↱] [PROB] [RAND] \(\rightarrow\) \(0.183022\) [↱] [PROB] [RAND] \(\rightarrow\) \(0.941581\).

    Because [↱] [PROB] [RAND] is a non-consuming generator that enables stack lift before pushing each uniform variate \(U_k \in [0, 1)\) into Register \(X\), three consecutive invocations populate a 3-dimensional Monte Carlo sample vector directly across Registers \(Z, Y,\) and \(X\): the first press generates \(U_1 = \mathbf{0.724194}\) in \(X\), the second lifts \(U_1 \to Y\) while placing \(U_2 = \mathbf{0.183022}\) in \(X\), and the third lifts \(U_1 \to Z, U_2 \to Y\) while placing \(U_3 = \mathbf{0.941581}\) in \(X\).

    Workbook Problem 10.22: Pseudo-Random Die Roll Simulation

    Workbook Problem 11.21: Cash Conversion Cycle (CCC)

    Days Inventory Outstanding \(DIO = 45\text{ days}\); Days Sales Outstanding \(DSO = 38\text{ days}\); Days Payables Outstanding \(DPO = 52\text{ days}\):

    \[\text{CCC} = DIO + DSO - DPO = 45 + 38 - 52 = \mathbf{31\text{ days}}\]

    In working-capital treasury management, the Cash Conversion Cycle measures how many days cash remains tied up in inventory and customer receivables beyond trade supplier credit terms: 45 [ENTER] 38 [+] 52 [-] resolves \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{31}\text{ days})\).

    Workbook Problem 11.22: Enterprise Value (EV) Calculation

    Market capitalization \(\$450\text{M}\), total debt \(\$120\text{M}\), cash and equivalents \(\$35\text{M}\):

    \[\text{EV} = \text{Market Cap} + \text{Debt} - \text{Cash} = 450 + 120 - 35 = \mathbf{\$535.00M}\]

    In mergers-and-acquisitions valuation, Enterprise Value represents the net takeover price of the operating business—acquiring all equity plus assuming gross debt, less liquid cash on the balance sheet (450 [ENTER] 120 [+] 35 [-] \(\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{\$535.00M})\)).

    Workbook Problem 11.25: Net Present Value (NPV) Comparison of Capital Projects

    A company evaluates two mutually exclusive industrial automation projects with a \(12.0\%\) hurdle rate: Project Alpha: Initial Cost \(\$80,000.00\); Inflows: \(\$35,000/\text{yr}\) for 3 years. Project Beta: Initial Cost \(\$120,000.00\); Inflows: \(\$52,000/\text{yr}\) for 3 years:

    $\(\text{PV Annuity Factor} = \frac{1 - (1.12)^{-3}}{0.12} = \frac{1 - 0.71178}{0.12} \approx 2.40183\)$ * Project Alpha: \(35,000 \times 2.40183 - 80,000 = \$84,064.10 - \$80,000 = \mathbf{+\$4,064.10}\). * Project Beta: \(52,000 \times 2.40183 - 120,000 = \$124,895.22 - \$120,000 = \mathbf{+\$4,895.22}\). Project Beta delivers \(\$831.12\) more net present value to shareholders.

    Because N, I%, and FV remain persistent across TVM evaluations, discounting Project Alpha (3 [N] 12 [I%] 35000 [+/-] [PMT] 0 [FV] [PV] \(\rightarrow \$84,064.10\), \((T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -35000.00, X: 84064.10)\), less 80000 [-] \(\rightarrow (T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -35000.00, X: \mathbf{+\$4064.10})\)) requires only updating PMT for Project Beta (52000 [+/-] [PMT] [PV] \(\rightarrow \$124,895.22\), \((T: 12.00, Z: -35000.00, Y: 4064.09, X: 124895.22)\), less 120000 [-] \(\rightarrow (T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -52000.00, X: \mathbf{+\$4895.22})\)).

    Workbook Problem 11.26: Capital Budgeting Profitability Index (PI)

    Using the present value of inflows from Workbook Problem 11.25:

    $\(\text{Profitability Index (PI)} = \frac{\text{PV of Future Inflows}}{\text{Initial Investment}}\)$ * Project Alpha: \(84,064.10 \div 80,000.00 = \mathbf{1.0508}\) (\(1.05\text{x}\)) * Project Beta: \(124,895.22 \div 120,000.00 = \mathbf{1.0408}\) (\(1.04\text{x}\)) Although Project Beta has higher absolute NPV, Project Alpha delivers higher capital efficiency per dollar invested!

    When capital rationing constrains total CapEx budget, ranking projects by Profitability Index \(\text{PI} = \text{PV}_{\text{inflows}}/I_0\) (84064.10 [ENTER] 80000 [÷] \(\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.0508}\text{x})\) for Alpha versus 124895.22 [ENTER] 120000 [÷] \(\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 1.05, X: \mathbf{1.0408}\text{x})\) for Beta) identifies which project generates more present value per dollar of scarce capital.

    Workbook Problem 12.4: High-Pass RC Filter Frequency Response

    The high-pass filter frequency response is given by:

    $\(|H(f)| = \frac{f / f_c}{\sqrt{1 + (f / f_c)^2}}\)$ For cutoff frequency \(f_c = 1000\text{ Hz}\): 1. Program in [↱] [EQN]: (X/1000)/SQRT(1+(X/1000)^2) 2. Stage domain (\(x_{\min}\) in \(Y\), \(x_{\max}\) in \(X\)): 0 [ENTER] 5000 3. Press [↱] [PLOT]. The curve begins at \(0\) at DC (\(f=0\)), crosses \(0.707107\) at \(f = 1000\text{ Hz}\), and asymptotically approaches \(1.00\) at high frequencies. Octave-Above-Cutoff Check (\(f = 2000\text{ Hz}\)): 2000 [ENTER] 1000 [÷] [STO] A [↰] [x²] 1 [+] [√x] [RCL] A [x⇄y] [÷] \(\implies \mathbf{0.894427}\).

    In a first-order RC high-pass section, the capacitor blocks DC (\(|H(0)| = 0\)) and passes high-frequency ac components above the corner frequency \(f_c = \frac{1}{2\pi R C} = 1000\text{ Hz}\). Evaluating the normalized frequency ratio at one octave above cutoff (2000 [ENTER] 1000 [÷] [STO] A \(\rightarrow f/f_c = 2.00\), \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.000000)\)), forming the impedance modulus \(\sqrt{1 + (f/f_c)^2} = \sqrt{5}\) via [↰] [x²] 1 [+] [√x] \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.236068)\), and dividing via [RCL] A [x⇄y] [÷] establishes the octave-above-cutoff attenuation factor \(2/\sqrt{5}\) \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{0.894427}\), or \(-0.97\text{ dB})\). (Zero-Register Stack Alternative: Duplicating the normalized frequency ratio on the stack via 2000 [ENTER] 1000 [÷] [ENTER] [↰] [x²] 1 [+] [√x] [÷] keeps \(f/f_c = 2.00\) waiting in Register \(Y\) while \(\sqrt{1 + (f/f_c)^2}\) forms in Register \(X\), resolving \(\mathbf{0.894427}\) without touching storage register A!)

    Workbook Problem 12.5: Diode Forward Bias I-V Characteristic (Shockley Equation)

    In solid-state semiconductor electronics, junction diode current follows:

    Workbook Problem 12.15: Gaussian Error Function (\(\text{erf}\)) Diffusion Profile

    In Fickian heat and mass diffusion, concentration across a semi-infinite slab follows the Gaussian error function:

    \[\text{erf}(x) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}} \int_0^x e^{-t^2} dt\]

    The sigmoidal curve transitions smoothly from \(-1.0\) at \(-\infty\) to \(+1.0\) at \(+\infty\), passing through \((0, 0)\) with central inflection slope \(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}} \approx \mathbf{1.128379}\) ([↱] [π] [√x] 2 [x⇄y] [÷]).

    Solving Fick's second law \(\frac{\partial C}{\partial t} = D \frac{\partial^2 C}{\partial z^2}\) via Boltzmann's similarity variable \(x = \frac{z}{2\sqrt{Dt}}\) yields the error-function profile whose steepest concentration gradient occurs at the interface \(x = 0\): evaluating [↱] [π] [√x] (\(\sqrt{\pi} \approx 1.772454\), \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.772454)\)), staging 2 [x⇄y] \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 2.000000, X: 1.772454)\), and dividing via [÷] establishes the Maclaurin leading-term slope \(2/\sqrt{\pi}\) \((T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.128379})\).

    Workbook Problem 12.16: Wien Displacement Blackbody Radiation Curve

    Planck's spectral radiance for a tungsten filament at \(T = 3000\text{ K}\) obeys:

    \[B(\lambda, T) \propto \frac{\lambda^{-5}}{e^{hc/(\lambda k_B T)} - 1}, \qquad \lambda_{\max} = \frac{b}{T}\]

    Workbook Problem 12.17: Jakob Bernoulli's Lemniscate

    Workbook Problem 13.1: Torricelli Efflux Tank Draining Velocity

    A cylindrical fluid storage tank maintains an open liquid column height \(h = 5.0\text{ meters}\). Standard acceleration of gravity \(g = 9.80665\text{ m/s}^2\). Determine the efflux velocity \(v\): $\(v = \sqrt{2 g h} = \sqrt{2 \times 9.80665 \times 5.0} = \sqrt{98.0665} \approx \mathbf{9.90285\text{ m/s}}\)$

    Keystrokes: 2 [ENTER] 9.80665 [×] 5 [×] [√x] \(\rightarrow\) 9.9028531242.

    Evangelista Torricelli's 1643 efflux theorem—a direct specialization of Daniel Bernoulli's streamline energy balance \(\rho g h = \frac{1}{2}\rho v^2\)—states that liquid jets exit a bottom orifice at the free-fall speed acquired across head height \(h\): evaluating 2 [ENTER] 9.80665 [×] 5 [×] (\(2gh = 98.0665\text{ m}^2/\text{s}^2\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 98.0665)\)) and extracting the square root via [√x] determines the discharge velocity \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{9.9029}\text{ m/s})\).

    Workbook Problem 13.2: Darcy-Weisbach Friction Head Loss in Piping

    Water at \(20^\circ\text{C}\) flows at velocity \(v = 2.40\text{ m/s}\) through a commercial steel pipe with length \(L = 85.0\text{ m}\) and inner diameter \(D = 0.15\text{ m}\).     Workbook Problem 13.4: Ideal Brayton Gas Turbine Cycle Thermal Efficiency

    Workbook Problem 13.6: Rankine Steam Power Cycle Thermal Efficiency

    Workbook Problem 13.7: Otto Cycle Internal Combustion Efficiency vs. Compression Ratio

    The theoretical thermal efficiency of an internal combustion Otto cycle engine depends solely on compression ratio \(r = V_{\max} / V_{\min}\) and air specific heat ratio \(\gamma = 1.40\): $\(\eta_{\text{Otto}} = 1 - \frac{1}{r^{\gamma - 1}}\)$ For a high-performance racing engine with compression ratio \(r = 11.5\): $\(\eta_{\text{Otto}} = 1 - \frac{1}{11.5^{0.40}} = 1 - \frac{1}{2.65839} = 1 - 0.376167 = \mathbf{62.35\%}\)$

    In Nikolaus Otto's four-stroke spark-ignition cycle, isentropic compression across volume ratio \(r = 11.5\) elevates the cycle temperature ratio to \(r^{\gamma-1}\): keying 11.5 [ENTER] .40 [yˣ] (\(11.5^{0.40} \approx 2.6563\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.6563)\)) and chaining [1/x] 1 [x⇄y] [-] determines the air-standard Otto efficiency \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.6235}\), or \(62.35\%)\).

    Workbook Problem 13.8: 2nd-Order Butterworth Crossover Filters

    Crossover frequency \(f_c = 2500\text{ Hz}\), driver nominal impedance \(R = 8.0\,\Omega\):

    \[L = \frac{R \sqrt{2}}{2\pi f_c} = \frac{8 \sqrt{2}}{2\pi \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{0.720253\text{ mH}}$$ $$C = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{2} R f_c} = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{2} \times 8 \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{5.62698\text{ }\mu\text{F}}\]

    Stephen Butterworth's maximally flat second-order crossover (\(Q = 1/\sqrt{2} \approx 0.7071\)) requires reactive impedance \(X_L = \sqrt{2}R\) and capacitive reactance \(X_C = R/\sqrt{2}\) at the crossover frequency \(f_c\): evaluating 8 [ENTER] 2 [√x] [×] 2 [÷] [↱] [π] [÷] 2500 [÷] determines the series woofer inductance \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{7.2025 \times 10^{-4}}\text{ H}\), or \(0.7203\text{ mH})\), and evaluating 2 [↱] [π] [×] 2 [√x] [×] 8 [×] 2500 [×] [1/x] determines the shunt tweeter capacitance \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 7.2025 \times 10^{-4}, X: \mathbf{5.6270 \times 10^{-6}}\text{ F}\), or \(5.63\,\mu\text{F})\)—or even faster, since \(C = \frac{L}{2 R^2} = \frac{L}{128}\), simply dividing \(L\) by 128 [÷] directly on the stack yields \(C = \mathbf{5.6270\,\mu\text{F}}\) in a single division!

    Workbook Problem 13.9: 4th-Order Linkwitz-Riley Crossover (\(24\text{ dB/octave}\))

    For \(f_c = 2500\text{ Hz}, R = 8.0\,\Omega\):

    \[L_{LR4} = \frac{R}{\pi f_c} = \frac{8.0}{\pi \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{1.01859\text{ mH}}$$ $$C_{LR4} = \frac{1}{4\pi R f_c} = \frac{1}{4\pi \times 8.0 \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{3.97887\text{ }\mu\text{F}}\]

    Workbook Problem 13.11: Zobel Network Dynamic Impedance Equalizer

    Workbook Problem 13.14: Thick Lens Equivalent Focal Length (Gullstrand Formula)

    A thick biconvex crown glass lens has front radius \(R_1 = 50.0\text{ mm}\), rear radius \(R_2 = -80.0\text{ mm}\), center thickness \(d = 12.0\text{ mm}\), and refractive index \(n = 1.520\). Gullstrand's lens maker formula states: $\(\frac{1}{f} = (n - 1) \left[ \frac{1}{R_1} - \frac{1}{R_2} + \frac{(n - 1) d}{n R_1 R_2} \right]\)$ 1. Front surface power: \((n - 1) / R_1 = 0.520 / 50 = 0.010400\text{ mm}^{-1}\). 2. Rear surface power: \(-(n - 1) / R_2 = -0.520 / (-80) = 0.006500\text{ mm}^{-1}\). 3. Thickness coupling term: $\(\frac{(n - 1)^2 d}{n R_1 R_2} = \frac{0.520^2 \times 12.0}{1.520 \times 50.0 \times (-80.0)} = \frac{3.2448}{-6080.0} = -0.00053368\text{ mm}^{-1}\)$ 4. Total optical power: \(P = 0.010400 + 0.006500 - 0.00053368 = 0.01636632\text{ mm}^{-1}\). 5. Equivalent focal length: \(f = 1 / P \approx \mathbf{61.10\text{ mm}}\).

    Workbook Problem 13.17: Gravitational Escape Velocity from Earth and Mars

    Planetary surface escape velocity is given by \(v_{\text{esc}} = \sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{r}}\): 1. Earth (\(\mu = 3.986004 \times 10^{14}\text{ m}^3/\text{s}^2, r = 6.378 \times 10^6\text{ m}\)): $\(v_{\text{esc},\oplus} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 3.986004 \times 10^{14}}{6.378 \times 10^6}} \approx \mathbf{11,180.00\text{ m/s}} \quad (\mathbf{11.18\text{ km/s}})\)$ 2. Mars (\(\mu_{\text{Mars}} = 4.282837 \times 10^{13}\text{ m}^3/\text{s}^2, r_{\text{Mars}} = 3.3895 \times 10^6\text{ m}\)): $\(v_{\text{esc,Mars}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 4.282837 \times 10^{13}}{3.3895 \times 10^6}} \approx \mathbf{5,026.91\text{ m/s}} \quad (\mathbf{5.03\text{ km/s}})\)$

    Setting total specific mechanical energy \(\mathcal{E} = \frac{1}{2}v^2 - \frac{\mu}{r} = 0\) on a parabolic escape trajectory yields \(v_{\text{esc}} = \sqrt{2\mu/r} = \sqrt{2}\,v_{\text{circ}}\): evaluating 2 [ENTER] 3.986004 [E] 14 [×] 6.378 [E] 6 [÷] (\(2\mu/r \approx 1.2500 \times 10^8\text{ m}^2/\text{s}^2\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.2500 \times 10^8)\)) and extracting the square root via [√x] determines Earth's surface escape velocity \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{11180.00}\text{ m/s}\), or \(11.18\text{ km/s})\).

    Workbook Problem 13.18: Meridian Convergence & True vs. Magnetic Azimuth

    Workbook Problem 13.19: Bowditch Compass Rule Linear Misclosure Balancing

    Workbook Problem 13.21: Cantilever Beam Maximum Tip Deflection Under Uniform Load

    Workbook Problem 13.22: Standard Gibbs Free Energy & Equilibrium Constant

    Workbook Problem 13.23: Nernst Electrochemical Cell Potential

    For a magnesium fluoride (\(n = 1.38\)) anti-reflective lens coating, a quarter-wave optical thickness \(n t = \lambda/4\) shifts the rear-surface reflection by half a wavelength (\(180^\circ\)) to cancel front-surface glare: dividing 5.5 [E] 7 [+/-] 4 [÷] (\(1.3750 \times 10^{-7}\text{ m}\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.3750 \times 10^{-7})\)) by 1.38 [÷] determines the film thickness \(t = \mathbf{9.9638 \times 10^{-8}\text{ m}}\) (\(9.96 \times 10^{-8}\text{ m}\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 9.9638 \times 10^{-8})\).

    Workbook Problem 14.31: Barometric Atmospheric Pressure at Altitude

    Sea level pressure \(P_0 = 101.325\text{ kPa}\), scale height \(H = 8,400\text{ m}\). Find pressure at cruising altitude \(h = 10,500\text{ m}\): \(P = P_0 e^{-h/H}\). Keystrokes: 10500 [+/-] [ENTER] 8400 [÷] [eˣ] 101.325 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 29.030098542 (3-sig-fig target: 29.0 (\(29.030\text{ kPa})\)).

    Under isothermal hydrostatic equilibrium \(\frac{dP}{dh} = -\frac{P}{H}\), evaluating the scale-height exponent 10500 [+/-] [ENTER] 8400 [÷] (\(-h/H = -1.2500\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -1.2500)\)), exponentiating via [eˣ] (\(0.2865\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.2865)\)), and scaling by standard atmosphere 101.325 [×] resolves \(P = \mathbf{29.0301\text{ kPa}}\) (\(29.0\text{ kPa}\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 29.0301)\).

    Workbook Problem 14.32: Chemical Equilibrium pH from Hydronium Ion

    \([H^+] = 3.45 \times 10^{-5}\text{ M}\). Find \(\text{pH} = -\log_{10}[H^+]\).

    Workbook Problem 14.33: Acoustics Sound Pressure Decibel Level

    Acoustic pressure \(P = 4.8 \times 10^{-4}\text{ Pa}\), reference \(P_0 = 20\text{ }\mu\text{Pa} = 2.0 \times 10^{-5}\text{ Pa}\). Find \(L_p = 20 \log_{10}(P / P_0)\). Keystrokes: 4.8 [E] 4 [+/-] [ENTER] 2 [E] 5 [+/-] [÷] [↰] [LOG] 20 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 27.604224834 (3-sig-fig target: 27.6 (\(27.604\text{ dB}\))).

    Dividing the RMS acoustic pressure by the \(20\,\mu\text{Pa}\) reference threshold 4.8 [E] 4 [+/-] [ENTER] 2 [E] 5 [+/-] [÷] (\(P/P_0 = 24.0000\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 24.0000)\)) and chaining [↰] [LOG] 20 [×] resolves \(L_p = \mathbf{27.6042\text{ dB}}\) (\(27.6\text{ dB}\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 27.6042)\).

    Workbook Problem 14.34: Golden Ratio Multi-Nested Radical

    Evaluate the infinite nested radical \(Q = \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \dots}}}} = \phi = \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}\).

    Keystrokes: 5 [√x] 1 [+] 2 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 1.6180339887 (3-sig-fig target: 1.62 (Full mantissa: 1.618033989)).

    Squaring both sides of the self-similar radical \(Q = \sqrt{1 + Q}\) yields the golden-ratio quadratic \(Q^2 - Q - 1 = 0\), whose positive root \(\phi = \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}\) evaluates in 4 tokens: 5 [√x] (\(2.2361\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.2361)\)) followed by 1 [+] 2 [÷] resolves \(\phi = \mathbf{1.6180}\) (\(1.62\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.6180)\).

    Workbook Problem 14.35: Surface Area of Irregular Chassis Solid via Stats Mode [Σ+]

    An engineering geometry problem asks for the total external surface area of a stepped rectangular chassis with outer dimensions: Length \(L = 135\text{ mm}\), Width \(W = 70\text{ mm}\), Height \(H = 16\text{ mm}\). The surface consists of three pairs of congruent rectangular faces: $\(A_{\text{total}} = 2(LW) + 2(LH) + 2(WH)\)$ Rather than computing each pair and manually adding, speed-calculation masters accumulate intermediate face areas directly into the statistical accumulator using [Σ+], then instantly recall total surface area via [Σx] (3-sig-fig target: 2.55E4, \(25,460\text{ mm}^2\)):

    STEP KEYSTROKE REGISTER T REGISTER Z REGISTER Y REGISTER X DISPLAY
    01 135 [ENTER] 70 [×] 2 [×] 0.00 0.00 0.00 18900.00 18900.00
    02 [Σ+] 0.00 0.00 0.00 1.0000(n=1) n=1
    03 135 [ENTER] 16 [×] 2 [×] 0.00 0.00 0.00 4320.000 4320.000
    04 [Σ+] 0.00 0.00 0.00 2.0000(n=2) n=2
    05 70 [ENTER] 16 [×] 2 [×] 0.00 0.00 0.00 2240.000 2240.000
    06 [Σ+] 0.00 0.00 0.00 3.0000(n=3) n=3

    Workbook Problem 14.36: Surface Area of Cylindrical Battery Pod via Stats Mode [Σ+]

    An applied physics problem asks for the total surface area of a cylindrical battery pod: Radius \(r = 15\text{ mm}\), Height \(h = 65\text{ mm}\):

    \[A_{\text{total}} = 2(\pi r^2) + 2\pi r h = 2\pi (15^2) + 2\pi(15)(65) = 450\pi + 1950\pi = 2400\pi \approx 7,539.82\text{ mm}^2\]

    Clearing the statistical registers ([↰] [CLEAR] [CLΣ]), accumulating the twin end-cap areas 15 [↰] [x²] [↱] [π] [×] 2 [×] [Σ+] (\(1,413.72\text{ mm}^2\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0000)\)) and lateral shell area 2 [↱] [π] [×] 15 [×] 65 [×] [Σ+] (\(6,126.11\text{ mm}^2\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)\)), and recalling [↱] [SUMS] [Σx] resolves the total surface area \(\Sigma x = \mathbf{7,539.82\text{ mm}^2}\) (3-sig-fig target: 7.54E3, \(7.54 \times 10^3\text{ mm}^2\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7539.8224)\)—or in factored form \(2\pi r(r + h)\), by keying \(h = 65\), adding \(r = 15\), and recalling \(r = 15\) from LASTX so the radius is entered only once.

    Workbook Problem 14.37: Constant Factor Replication in Register T for Repeated Pricing

    In repetitive commercial applications, users must apply a constant sales tax rate \(8.25\%\) (multiplier \(1.0825\)) across three consecutive item prices: \(\$10.00, \$25.00, \$150.00\). On the SC-32, loading the constant into Register \(T\) allows perpetual reuse because when a dyadic drop occurs, Register \(T\) drops to \(Z\) and replicates itself in \(T\)!

    Target Answers:
    - Price 1 (\(10.00\)): 10.8 (\(10.825\))
    - Price 2 (\(25.00\)): 27.1 (\(27.0625\))
    - Price 3 (\(150.00\)): 162 (\(162.375\))
    Keystrokes: 1. Fill stack with constant: 1.0825 [ENTER] [ENTER] [ENTER]
    2. Price 1: 1.0825 [ENTER] 10 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 10.825 (Register \(T\) retains \(1.0825\))
    3. Price 2: [CLx] 25 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 27.0625 (Register \(T\) retains \(1.0825\))
    4. Price 3: [CLx] 150 [×] \(\rightarrow\) 162.375 (Register \(T\) perpetually retains \(1.0825\))

    STEP KEYSTROKE REGISTER T REGISTER Z REGISTER Y REGISTER X DISPLAY
    01 1.0825 [ENTER] [ENTER] [ENTER] 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825
    02 10 [×] 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825 10.8250 10.8250
    03 [CLx] 25 [×] 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825 27.0625 27.0625
    04 [CLx] 150 [×] 1.0825 1.0825 1.0825 162.3750 162.3750

    Workbook Problem 14.38: Unit Conversion via Constant Factor in Register T

    Convert three dimension measurements given in inches to millimeters using the exact conversion constant \(25.4\text{ mm/in}\): \(2.0\text{ in}, 5.0\text{ in}, 10.0\text{ in}\). Target Answers:
    - \(2.0\text{ in}\): 50.8 \(\text{mm}\)
    - \(5.0\text{ in}\): 127 \(\text{mm}\)
    - \(10.0\text{ in}\): 254 \(\text{mm}\)

    Loading the SI conversion factor across all four stack tiers via 25.4 [ENTER] [ENTER] [ENTER] \((T: 25.4000, Z: 25.4000, Y: 25.4000, X: 25.4000)\) turns Register \(T\) into a non-depleting constant source: 2 [×] resolves 50.8000 mm \((T: 25.4000, Z: 25.4000, Y: 25.4000, X: 50.8000)\), [CLx] 5 [×] resolves 127.0000 mm \((T: 25.4000, Z: 25.4000, Y: 25.4000, X: 127.0000)\), and [CLx] 10 [×] resolves 254.0000 mm \((T: 25.4000, Z: 25.4000, Y: 25.4000, X: 254.0000)\) without re-entering 25.4.

    Workbook Problem 14.39: Infix Operator Precedence vs Native RPN Stack Equivalence

    Compare algebraic infix parsing requiring nested parentheses with natural RPN execution for two standard tournament expressions: 1. \((12 - 4) \times (6 + 2) \div 4 = \mathbf{16.0}\)
    2. \(3 + 4 \times 5 = \mathbf{23.0}\)

    RPN Keystrokes:
    - Expression 1: 12 [ENTER] 4 [-] 6 [ENTER] 2 [+] [×] 4 [÷] \(\rightarrow\) 16.0 (Evaluates intermediate differences and sums directly on the stack without a single parenthesis)
    - Expression 2: 3 [ENTER] 4 [ENTER] 5 [×] [+] \(\rightarrow\) 23.0 (Multiplying 4 and 5 first, then adding 3, completely mirrors operator precedence without requiring parentheses).

    In Jan Łukasiewicz's postfix logic, operator position alone encodes tree topology without delimiters: evaluating 12 [ENTER] 4 [-] (\(8.0000\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 8.0000)\)), 6 [ENTER] 2 [+] (\(8.0000\) in \(X\) with \(8.0000\) lifted to \(Y\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 8.0000, X: 8.0000)\)), and [×] 4 [÷] yields \(\mathbf{16.0000}\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 16.0000)\), while 4 [ENTER] 5 [×] 3 [+] evaluates \(\mathbf{23.0000}\) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 16.0000, X: 23.0000)\).

    Workbook Problem 14.40: Nested Horner's Method for Cubic Polynomials

    Evaluate \(P(x) = 3x^3 - 5x^2 + 4x - 7\) at \(x = 2.5\). Speed Optimization Insight: Evaluating polynomials term-by-term with powers (\(x^3, x^2\)) wastes time and invites syntax errors. Rewriting in nested Horner form \(((3x - 5)x + 4)x - 7\) allows continuous left-to-right streaming on the stack with zero parentheses:
    Keystrokes: 3 [ENTER] 2.5 [×] 5 [-] 2.5 [×] 4 [+] 2.5 [×] 7 [-] \(\rightarrow\) 18.625 (3-sig-fig target: 18.6 (Full mantissa: 18.625)).

    William George Horner's 1819 nested synthetic division reduces a degree-\(n\) polynomial to \(n\) multiply-add pairs: evaluating 3 [ENTER] 2.5 [×] 5 [-] (\(3x - 5 = 2.5000\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.5000)\)), chaining 2.5 [×] 4 [+] (\((3x - 5)x + 4 = 10.2500\), \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 10.2500)\)), and finishing with 2.5 [×] 7 [-] resolves \(P(2.5) = \mathbf{18.6250}\) (\(18.6\)) \((T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 18.6250)\)—and by pre-filling the stack with \(x = 2.5\) (2.5 [ENTER] [ENTER] [ENTER] 3 [×] 5 [-] [×] 4 [+] [×] 7 [-]), Register \(T\) feeds \(2.5\) into every Horner stage automatically so 2.5 is typed only once!
